#10353. Playing GTA 6 this weekend? Here's 16 spoiler-free tips for your first playthrough

It's launch weekend for GTA 6 - in case you hadn't noticed - and since I've played the best part of a hundred hours of this thing, I thought it might be useful to share a few pieces of advice from my time with it.

Many of these are most applicable for the early game, but there are a few that'll be worth bearing in mind throughout. After last night's driving around I would also maybe add in a quick bonus one, come to think of it: watch out for cyclists making last-minute left turns. Or don't, actually. Half of this lot deserve it…

The below list is spoiler-free, so you can relax if you're anxious about going through the story entirely fresh. Though naturally I do explain the way some parts of this game work, taking away some of the sense of discovery. You can take my word for it that there's plenty more to discover beyond this though, so worry not.

If you'd like even more help navigating Leonida, then check out our wonderful guide team's GTA 6 walkthrough.

The number one thing I'd tell people to do in GTA 6 is to create lots of save files and, as you progress and start needing to overwrite them, to try to keep a roughly even spread of files through each chapter. There are various reasons for this - for one, while the game autosaves, it doesn't seem to do that successfully if you suddenly lose power to the console or witness a rare game crash, so it'll preserve your progress. More than that: GTA's story and missions are surprisingly, perhaps mysteriously reactive. Quite a lot can differ, and so being able to go back and make different choices, or play the game differently, will be something 'future you' likely appreciates.

In the very early game you won't have access to a bank account, but once you do: you should put every bit of cash you have in there as soon as you can. Cash is lost on death or arrest - which can happen quite often in GTA 6, funnily enough - and there's no real benefit to carrying it around. Just about everything, besides in-game strippers, can be paid for via your BuckMe account directly. And there's a relatively low $20,000 limit you really don't want to hit in the middle of raiding a gang hideout, either.

GTA 6 has a luxurious range of customisation settings, from hiding notifications about your relationship to reinstating the old 'tap to sprint' control scheme. The accessibility settings - while crucially I'm not an expert - seem considerable. And there are somewhat disguised difficult settings here around enemy damage taken and encounters with the police.

Fairly early in the game you'll gain access to the Scooter Bros. App, which lets you repair or summon your owned vehicles at will. Crucially, there's also an option to pay a one-off fee to unlock three free uses of the app per day. At $12,000, in the early game this is a lot of dosh, but once you rack up that kind of money after advancing through the game a little, it's well worth the initial cost. The sooner you unlock it, the more value it provides over time on a cost-per-use basis. And trust me when I say: as the game's intensity ramps up, you will probably find yourself needing to repair and recover your vehicles quite often. (Also, in Chapter 1, before you get access to the app, you can access the same service by going to your phone's contacts and calling Joey - handy!)

Rockstar has somewhat disincentivised it by adding a cost and putting it in your phone's apps rather than the main map screen, but you can fast travel around Leonida if you ever want or need to. This is done via the RideMe Uber-like app. It's not cheap - you'll need to pay both the fee for the journey and an additional $100 to skip that journey and instantly arrive at your destination, but in certain scenarios it might prove useful. To skip the journey, hold L2/LT once in the cab, and then hold down Triangle/Y when the option is shown. However…

While it's possible to journey-skip your way around this world, having tested that first hand I'd advise against it. It might be obvious, but GTA 6 is genuinely designed around you manually travelling around, typically in the car. If you skip all your commutes between missions or locations, you'll miss out on the game's dozens and dozens of emergent events, which lead to anything from one-off jokes to entire mission chains. The car is also, in a way, kind of the heart of the entire game. Relish the intentional slowness!

Again, avoiding spoilers here, but in the mid game the 'true' loop of GTA 6 gameplay will properly reveal itself, in all the many methods of earning money and the increased emphasis on doing so. You can of course play the game however you like, but having reached the end and looked back from that point, I found that if there was a particular moment to set aside as the time for aimlessly exploring the world, it was from around Chapter 3 onwards. You'll have enough tools to be able to see it at more or less its fullest then, but also won't have missed anything essential by waiting until that point to take your foot off the pedal either.

A handy early-game tip here that even I missed when I was playing, but have since discovered: two islands in the small cluster of them to the south-west of Jason's early game home each house a really useful weapon, in a silenced pistol and a handy shotgun. You'll own these permanently once found, and so they can be incredibly helpful for navigating missions with maximum or minimal violence. Look for shipwrecks on those islands to find them. The pistol is hidden under a little hatch on the deck of a shipwreck on the southernmost point of the south-easternmost island. The shotgun is next to a small wooden wrecked boat around the middle of the northernmost island.

A mechanic I slightly underused at first was the ability to 'save' a given outfit to your car. You can do this either from your car's trunk or from the wardrobe, and it's hugely useful as you start to get into more elaborate scrapes with the police (and also if you just like to role-play a bit and don the right outfit for the right occasion).

There are lots of reasons for this, from the moments of intimacy it affords you with your partner, to the extra buffs you can get from resting. Sleep at home, for instance, and you can then immediately pet your snake (ahem), down a coffee or drink from the fridge, grab an apple and take a shower for a range of buffs across health, stamina, and focus. Each of these will last quite some time, so it's worth the few minutes of time to do them. Eating and drinking regularly also helps you maximise gains in the gym.

Rockstar seems to enjoy the concept of a game within a game, and that's exemplified well by the in-game phone. You can find missions via scrolling Snapmatic, it's in-game social media, or from texting various contacts you pick up as the game progresses. It's worth checking it every now and then just to see if anything interesting - or just funny or absurd - might pop up.

Car trunks, both those belonging to the public and the police, are often well worth a look. Even if you're quickly nabbing a car for a short-term dash, take a moment to check the boot for anything worthwhile. We've found everything from sellable narcotics to very useful weapons in there. In cop cars, that goes up several notches, too.

A small thing, but in-mission checkpoints are relatively generous in GTA 6. If you really wanted to complete a mission a certain way - undetected or non-violent, say - then don't be afraid to quickly re-load rather than feeling you need to manually load up an earlier save, or give up on your goal entirely. It's also helped me out with a few minor progress-jamming bugs along the way too.

This might seem obvious, but it's worth emphasising for early-game players: it's very, very rare that you actually need to kill anybody in GTA 6. Enemies are fully incapacitated when you see a yellow hit marker over your crosshairs, even if that just comes from blasting away at their toes. If you make a habit of navigating combat this way then that also makes it much easier to recover your 'Professional' criminal status if you ever do decide to go full rampage. Though you can, of course, ultimately play this game however you like. Just be aware there may be consequences, known and unknown, as you go. Also: don't forget to click the right analog stick to aim down sights - it's an easy thing to forget when playing a third-person shooter like this, and not always the best way to operate, but incredibly helpful in select circumstances.

Throughout the game you'll have a 'family savings' goal to hit. As you progress you'll find more ways to earn money much faster, and so it's easy to make up for lost time here if, later on, you decide that actually you do want to think about the future after all. But that grind will be a fair bit less painful if you chip away at the target as you go. As long as you save yourself some fun money - a few hundred in the early game, few thousand in the mid game, and a few tens of thousands late on - then you'll have enough to do key things like buying new garages to register vehicles, or paying to register them themselves.

Throughout your time with GTA 6 you'll encounter moments when you need to make a choice. Interestingly, I noticed at least a few occasions where a scenario changed when I just waited, doing nothing at all. Sometimes that might mean a choice is made for you - so waiting is a risk - but on other occasions new options become available. Sometimes they might mean a genuinely different consequence, others they might just lead to characters reacting in a certain kind of way. But it's worth experimenting - your first options aren't always your only ones.

#10351. Motorola Razr Flex leaks in real life with Fold 8 and iPhone Duo shape, mystery chipset

Motorola’s upcoming Razr Flex foldable is expected to have the same wide foldable form factor as Galaxy Z Fold 8 and iPhone Duo, and new leaks not only reveal its design, but hint at some key specs.

Ahead of its supposed 2027 debut, the Motorola Razr Flex has now leaked in a real-life image. The image shows the back of the phone with a dual-camera setup, Pixel-like camera bar, and a design that matches that short and stout look of Galaxy Z Fold 8 and iPhone Duo. It also pretty clearly lines up with past teasers of this device.

Rumor has it that the Razr Flex will be even smaller than Samsung or Apple’s wide foldables, and the proportions of this image make that all the more certain.

Beyond this, some regulatory leaks reveal key specs on the Motorola Razr Flex.

The foldable will apparently run Android 17 out of the box – an update that Razr Fold is only getting just now – and run on the Qualcomm SM8845 Pro chipset. Android Authority notes that this isn’t a chip that’s been announced yet, but it’s thought to be called “Snapdragon 8 Gen 5 Pro.”

Other rumored specs include a 4.97-inch outer display, 6.92-inch inner display, 5,000 mAh battery, 12GB of RAM, and two 50MP cameras around back. Based on information from a past leak, the color variant pictured above is likely called “Coriander.”

#10350. I completed GTA 6's indie game darling pastiche, Tenebrae Lux, and all I got was this lousy sense of accomplishment

"We are but Promethean wanderers on the fringes of oblivion, clinging to a semblance of light amidst the vast abyss of our own ignorance. Voyaging uncharted waters may appear foolish to some, yet folly and heroism can often appear the same to the undiscerning eye," so says the voice of Johannes Zuch - creator, writer, director, composer, and 'perceptual architect' behind Leonida's latest favourite indie darling art game, Tenebrae Lux. It's pretentious, it's deeply up its own arse, and it's awkward and off-putting to play. A must-review for Eurogamer.

Developed, seemingly single-handedly, by an eccentric developer called Johannes Zuch - auteur, pseud, charlatan - you first get introduced to this curious little experiment in one of GTA 6's earliest missions, where protagonist Jason is forced to play it during a 'lad's night in' with buddy Cal. Jason is quick to dismiss Tenebrae Lux in company, but once Cal slinks off home - hungover and dreary - I slide the disc back into my JoyStation console and sink my teeth back into it. Jason loves this stuff, really. He only keeps Time Jackers: Rewind installed so his mates don't judge him.

The first thing I notice about Tenebrae Lux is the name. It's Latin for 'darkness and light', or something similar. Is this riffing on Clair Obscur, which is French for 'light dark'? No, it's too cerebral for that - too plodding and navel-gazey. Sandfall Interactive wouldn't be this pretentious. Maybe it's a pastiche of Dear Esther, then, drawing on the tenets and schema of the 'walking simulator' genre and using that washed-out film noir aesthetic. But then, it's too wordy for that, with too many interactive and puzzle-adjacent elements. Ah, it must be mocking The Witness, instead - there's the telltale lighthouse, the 'I'm definitely not telling you where to go and what to do' internal narration, and of course the fingerprints of Johannes Zuch are all over it. Johannes Zuch, Jonathan Blow - see how they braided them together? (Sorry).

Whilst this has been created by Rockstar as a pastiche - as much loving send-up as it is withering take down - and designed to make a quick joke in the early 'Feng Shui' mission, the game is playable from start to finish if you so desire. You boot it up by inserting a physical disc, too, which is funny given the context. And it's good! Well, not good good, but it's a proper brainteaser, and ribs 2016's The Witness with such knowledge and (I think?) respect that it's hard not to be charmed by Rockstar's game-within-a-game. There are collectibles to be found that riff on Mark Z. Danielewski's House of Leaves, too, a knowing nod to the most try-hard of all try-hard literature.

Within seconds of the title screen, too, you're presented with a game-within-a-game-within-a-game. You must clear four boards filled with puzzle pieces as the protagonist mutters something mysterious and weird in his posh voice. Do so, and you unlock the door to the next location. Head up to a bunker, and you'll see a room filled with various clues - maybe it's a flashing lamp flickering in morse code, maybe it's a malfunctioning clock presenting a maths puzzle, maybe it's a pricetag on a record that changes on every visit, maybe it's a photograph with anomalous elements, maybe it's a "Developed Using Zuchworks Engine" easter egg hidden in the environment. You need to keep your wits about you, because one of these clues changes every time you enter - and the clue corresponds to a date that changes on a calendar on the wall.

You can look up a guide if you need to, or you can spend far too long figuring out how all this relates to the telephone you can find in the room on your own, like muggins here, before opening up the path to the next puzzle. Look at the image below to see the level of record-keeping and brainteasing this bizarre little game prompted from me. I will say, the sense of achievement you get from playing around with this stuff is pretty remarkable, given - let's not forget I'm actually playing Grand Theft Auto 6 whilst I'm wandering around in the black and white murk trying to complete a puzzle game for no actual reward.

The bunker room is, by far, the hardest part of Tenebrae Lux. From here, things ease up: you need to figure out the right sequence of glyphs on a bridge, and then put some distance between yourself and a curious and rather creepy suited man that's seemingly figuring things out ahead of you in a location inspired by, I think, The Backrooms (or Portal, or Superliminal, or maybe something else entirely. Who knows what Zuch was thinking?)

"With the last enigma finally mastered," the narrator drones in his bumbling monotone, "I step forward into the void, the shadows swallow, consuming all, and then finally the sweet embrace of darkness of which there is no end." 'You have just experienced a Johannes Zuch experience', Tenebrae Lux tells me, before cutting to the best joke of the whole thing: the credits roll much akin to something Hideo Kojima would produce.

So, what do you get out of all of this? Well - just like real puzzle games in the real world - nothing, really. If you've not completed one of the other three fully playable games in GTA 6, you will get a trophy/achievement. But given the main game is all about Jason and Lucia's desperate scramble to escape the poverty trap, to try and break free of the cycle of debt, violence, and consequence that keeps them clinging to Leonida's seedy underbelly, there's no monetary reward. There's no 'progress' in the sense of the protagonist's shared story.

Even in terms of your own cold, hard, mathematical 'completion' you only need to boot each of the four games up once in order to work towards GTA 6's 100% completion. So what's the point of all this? Why did I spend the better part of 90 minutes trying to figure out how to find a sliver of light in the dark in Tenebrae Lux? The game is a wonderful pastiche in that, essentially, it's not really saying anything. You can probably project some vague meaning onto it if you wish - I'm pretty certain the protagonist literally 'finds himself' at the end - but glib little readings like that are ten-a-penny in this genre.

I think the most impressive thing about Tenebrae Lux is that it exists. You're teased with it in an early mission - Rockstar may as well say 'look, we put a game in a game, lol' - but it's more than that. This is what happens when Rockstar's 4700-plus developers are given the room to… develop. Tenebrae Lux could very easily have been a non-interactive experience, a quick, cheap joke shown over-the-shoulder taking a drive-by pop shot at Johnathan Blow, Hideo Kojima, Dan Pinchbeck, or any and all of the above. Or maybe it could be a 'vertical slice', an incomplete demo, a teaser. But it's not, because nothing about GTA 6 is quick or cheap.

GTA 6, in every single conceivable way, is a premium experience - part-sandbox, part-simulation, part-otherworld. To create not just one, but four fully playable and completable games within that framework is showboating, sure, but to make that game actually good and - perhaps controversially - even better than some of the games it's pastiching… Well, it's just rubbing salt in the wound, really.

A code for the game was provided by Cal Hampton.
